● The design bible is on main, corrected and checked: two documents and an index
  under .mdmd/layer-2/design-system/, with the findings filed in the day's
  Probes/design-system/. Writing it found fifteen things that are yours to
  choose.

  Below is each with my recommendation. As with any numbered list from me, what
  you don't mention stands as recommended. Nothing visual becomes a default
  before you have seen it in the app.

  The ones I'd act on

  1. Default palette. The Explorer opens in "Today", which fails the product's
     own colour test: an offering pin and an implementation file are the same
     blue, and three text greys are too faint. Recommend: make Vivid kinds the
     default. It is your own lean, built to pass every floor, and it fixes the
     faint text at once. It is in Tuning → Colour now if you want to look first.
  2. A shared link changes the receiver's saved settings. A link carries the
     sender's layout and filters, and the next time the receiver touches any
     setting, the sender's values are saved as theirs. You said "Opening a sent
     link shouldn't cause your local settings to change." Recommend: fix it, so
     that a link's layout is part of what the link shows and is never saved.
  3. The 3D camera cannot be turned without a drag. Recommend: arrow keys, with
     no new buttons on screen.
  4. The World Map's wires flow forever, with no pause. Recommend: one pause
     control among its tools, rather than stopping the flow after a few seconds,
     since you love that motion.
  5. Glows. A test-backed card and the centred card both glow from below, though
     you asked for glows only on pins and changed files. Recommend: drop those
     two and let the border and the chip say "has tests". I'll show it before
     and after.
  6. The scales. There are 16 font sizes a pixel apart, 14 corner sizes, 16
     shadows, and icons in two families at five sizes. No single one is wrong;
     together the product looks made at different times. Recommend: adopt short
     named scales one property at a time, corners first, each with pictures.

  Small, and I'd just do them

  7. Two loading signs at once: keep the dots.
  8. Our words in three different cases: sentence case everywhere.
  9. Enter on a card opens the reader while a click does nothing: drop the
     Enter, since the name and the book are the route.
  10. The cue's amber sits close to a directory's orange: move the amber.
  11. "Circles are things, pills are choices", but the zoom buttons are round:
      rewrite the rule to say what the product does.
  12. Directory corners are rounder than a card's, with no reason on record:
      keep them, and write the reason down as "rounder means container".

  Leave for now

  13. Floating panels are darker than the map, against the written rule.
      Recommend: rewrite the rule to match the product, unless the dark panels
      bother you.
  14. A click reads a file in 2D and pins it in 3D. Both are your decisions.
      Recommend: leave them and let the blind playtests show whether anyone
      slips.
  15. White as the history's lit file. It reads as unpainted among marbles.
      Recommend: decide it with the marble trial.

  The full wording of each, with what a visitor sees today and what each choice
  costs, is in the README of Probes/design-system/ under "The owner's forks".

  - owner.md: its second draft is back at the same size as the original (162

    and facts, so its gain is the grouping by subject and not the size.
  - ideas.md: its draft is back at 94 KB against 98 KB. Ten ideas that have

  run | not yet run |

  two App.config links. Both Haiku runs read the answer from the file's reader
  (its Dependents list), which no stronger model opened.
  - **The link:** all eight searched, opened the file and copied the
  726-character address from `look`. Each copy matches the address exactly, and
  the address opens on ServiceRouting.cs. So this task measures finding the
  file, not reading an address.

  nearly always the cycle. Per-run reasons, with action numbers and the testers'
  own words, are in the README.

  ladder runs alone cost about $1.37.

  ## The stuck points, grouped into four design questions
  Picture paths are relative to the records folder.

  1. **The view does not frame what the visitor came for.** Arriving on a pinned
  file cuts its neighbours off on both sides. Opening a folder leaves the top
  of the view empty and pushes its files off the bottom. Pressing `-` four times
  grows and wraps the names ("Intern als") and still leaves the right side cut
  off. Fit is what works.
     - Every rung hit it. All 8 estate-uses runs changed the view first, 4 of
  them by detours through `-` and drags. 9 of 10 estate-directory runs pressed
  fit after opening Data; Haiku 2 didn't and missed what lay below.
     - Pictures: `estate-uses-opus-low-look-only-2/shots/001-look.png`,
  `estate-uses-opus-low-look-only-2/shots/006-click.png`,
  `estate-directory-haiku-default-look-only-2/shots/007-click.png`.
     - Question: should arriving at a subject and opening a folder frame them,
  so fit becomes a recovery rather than a step everyone must know? And what is
  `-` for? This is the product's, since Opus at medium effort took the same
  detours.
  2. **A World Map box does not show the folder it opens.** "payments" opens
  PaymentService/.
     - Every estate-directory run hit it. Opus took the box on trust in all 6
  runs and wrote "likely" in 4. Sonnet 1 and Haiku 2 searched instead ("Your
  link had no folder called "PaymentService" on the World Map"). Haiku 1 found
  the name on the card that one click opens.
     - Pictures: `estate-directory-opus-low-look-only/shots/001-look.png`,
  `estate-directory-haiku-default-look-only/shots/003-look.png`,
  `estate-directory-sonnet-medium-look-only/shots/004-look.png`.
     - Question: should the box show the folder name? This is the product's.
  3. **A cycle's returning connection is drawn as a stub and read as a loop on a
  file to itself.**
     - 5 of 10 estate-directory runs read it that way: three of four Opus medium
  runs, Opus low 1 and Haiku 1. Sonnet never did.
     - Pictures: `estate-directory-opus-medium-look-only-2/shots/006-click.png`,
  `estate-directory-opus-medium-after-each-2/shots/005-point.png`.
     - Question: where does the returning wire go, and how does the drawing name
  the file it comes back from? This is the product's.
  4. **Wires carry no words, and the plain answer sits one door away in the
  reader.** Testers read each wire's direction from the legend's colours and
  said so as a caveat: "the connections are only as exact as the lines I could
  trace"; "a few arrows could be the other way around". Both Haiku runs instead
  found the reader's Dependents list, which names the four files in text.
     - Pictures: `estate-uses-haiku-default-look-only-2/shots/008-click.png`,
  `estate-uses-sonnet-medium-look-only/shots/002-click.png`.
     - Question: should a wire say what it carries and which way it goes, on
  hover or on the card, and should the reader's Dependents and Dependencies be
  easier to reach? The stronger models' hedges are the drawing's fault. Haiku's
  vaguer readings ("connect to each other") are the model's.

  Faults of the models, not the product: Sonnet asked once for a `double_click`
  tool it doesn't have, and Opus low wrote "Five files" while listing four.

  ## What I changed on the way, and what is unfinished
  - **The chained start for stage two never fired.** Its `pgrep -f` wait matched
  its own shell, the trap the tooling guide warns about. That cost about ten
  minutes of an idle lane before I noticed. I started the stage directly
  instead.
  - **Two open forks, both written in the README for the owner:**
    - `look` gives the address as text, so the link tasks cannot fail by
  misreading it. Drawing an address bar into the picture would change that.
    - Whether weaker models need a picture after every action waits on the 7
  after-each runs.
  - **My judging helpers** (`story.mjs`, `links.mjs`, `numbers.mjs`) are scratch
  files, not in the repository: `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentatio
  n/6450141c-9e11-40e5-95a5-166121c231a3/scratchpad/`. I kept them for the
  second half. They may not survive a cleanup of scratch space.

  Where they got stuck

  I looked at the cited screenshots for the first and third of these myself and
  see what the testers saw.

  1. The view does not frame what the visitor came for. Arriving on a pinned
     file, its neighbours are cut off at both edges. All 8 "what uses this file"
     runs had to change the view before they could answer, half of them by
     detours through zoom-out and dragging. After opening a folder, 9 of 10 runs
     pressed Fit. Opus at medium effort detoured too, so this is the product's
     fault and not a weak model's. It is the same thing as your wish tonight
     that the camera follow a newly opened cell.
  2. A World Map box does not name the folder it opens. "payments" opens
     PaymentService/. Every run met it: Opus trusted the box and wrote "likely",
     and two of the smaller-model runs went to search instead.
  3. A cycle's returning connection reads as a file linking to itself. Half the
     runs read "1 connection returns in a cycle" with its small loop that way.
     You noted on November 13 that it "looks like a mistake when zoomed out".
  4. Wires say nothing when pointed at. Testers inferred direction from the
     legend's colours and hedged their answers. Both Haiku runs found the plain
     answer in the reader's Dependents list, which no stronger model opened.

  One thing in the testers' favour is the harness's doing, not the product's:
  the link task cannot fail by misreading an address, because the tester is
  given the address bar as text.

  What I propose to do with it

  - Item 1 presses on a decision of yours from November 4: arrive on one card at
    reading size, neighbours cut, and learn by zooming out. I won't simply
    reverse it. I'd build the alternative behind a lever (arrive with the
    subject's neighbours' names in view), run the same testers on both, and
    bring you the numbers and both pictures in the app.
  - Items 2 and 4 I'd just build. The folder's name goes under the box's name,
    and a 2D wire says "X uses Y" under the pointer, as a 3D wire already does.
    Both show the reader's own words, and neither changes a decision.
  - Item 3 needs a design round with real alternatives for you to look at.

  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-a56f13063bbac6a0c`,

  regenerated, the new browser case has not run, and picture 4 (the 3D map of
  pathfind.ts) exists in "One Pattern" only. The shared browser lock was held
  past my time.

  ### The lever
  Tuning → Colour → **Marble Pattern**:
  - **One Pattern** (`one`, the default): today's marble.
  - **From Its Doc** (`doc`): the veins are bent by the sketch; the axis still
  comes from the path.
  - **From Its Doc, Axis Too** (`doc-axis`): the axis and the turn of the vanes
  come from the sketch too.

  The help sentence is "How a marble's veins wind: one pattern for all, or bent
  by its file's symbols and uses, so that a change shifts them."

  It is the viewer's own setting, `appearance.marblePattern`, saved in the
  browser and never in a link, the same rule as Marbles. That is why
  `layout-presets.ts` is untouched. The pattern state lives in `palette.ts`
  beside `marblesDrawn` and is heard by the same listener. The type
  `MarblePattern` lives in `palette.ts` and `types.ts` re-exports it, because
  `palette.ts` is compiled in a project that can't import `types.ts`.

  ### The sketch: `packages/explorer/src/client/views/marbleSketch.ts`
  - **Decided fork: which features.** I used only facts that both today's graph
  and a step of the history carry:
    - one feature per public symbol, keyed by the file's path and the symbol's
  place in the count;
    - one feature per file it uses, weighted √(references);
    - the file's own path, at the weight of one symbol.
  - **Why not symbol names:** the history keeps counts, not names. Names today
  and counts in the history would make every marble jump at the scrubber's last
  step, which shows today's graph and not a snapshot.
  - **Checked:** in the history sample, all 7 files that stand at the last step
  and today have identical sketches.
  - **Decided fork: what uses a file is not a feature.** Its own Live Doc
  doesn't list its users, and the blue vein already counts them.
  - Each feature's direction comes from FNV-1a hashing into a small random
  generator, 8 normal numbers brought to unit length. Features are summed in
  text order, so order can't matter.

  **Unit tests:** `marbleSketch.test.ts` has 8 cases. The client and script
  suites stand at 111 files and 1,234 tests, all passing. Over 300 random
  40-feature files, the mean distance rises with the share of features changed:

  | Share changed | 0 | 2.5% | 10% | 25% | 50% | 75% | 100% |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | Mean distance | 0 | 0.222 | 0.451 | 0.711 | 0.995 | 1.209 | 1.389 |

  For a file of 40 symbols, one symbol added moves the sketch by 0.103 and five
  by 0.355.

  `palette.test.ts` gained three things:
  - a guard check that allows only balanced inner `MARBLE_SKETCH` /
  `MARBLE_SKETCH_AXIS` guards;
  - a check that nothing of the sketch survives with `MARBLE_SKETCH` undefined;
  - a check that the pattern choice tells the palette's listeners.

  I also confirmed by hand that with One Pattern all five shader chunks are
  character-for-character today's.

  ### The shader
  - **Waves:** `MARBLE_WAVES` in `forceGraphAppearance.ts` replaces the old warp
  with W(p) = 0.55 · Σ sᵢ · sin(fᵢ·dᵢ·p + φᵢ), using 8 fixed directions and
  frequencies from 2.3 to 5.3.
  - **Attributes:** the sketch rides as two new vec4 instance attributes,
  `spatialMarbleSketchA` and `spatialMarbleSketchB`. `MARBLE_VALUES` goes from 7
  to 15, and `marbleValuesOf` now fills them for both the mesh spheres and the
  shell spheres.
  - **Defines:** `compileMarbles` sets `MARBLE_SKETCH` and `MARBLE_SKETCH_AXIS`
  only while the trial is chosen.
  - **Unchanged:** vanes, shares, edge filtering and the fade to the far tint.
  - **View:** `forceGraphView.ts` reads each file's sketch from the graph shown,
  today's or the step's.
  - **Not covered:** the 2D map's cells (under `localView/`, not mine) still
  draw one pattern.

  12.4 |

  - **`pathfind-3d-one.png`:** the 3D map of pathfind.ts in One Pattern only;
  its "From Its Doc" twin was not drawn.

  ### What the history can and cannot show
  Each step carries, per file, its kind, its symbol count, its entry mark, and
  its edges with reference counts. So a marble bends at a step where the file
  gained or lost a symbol, gained or lost a use, or changed how much it
  references a file it uses. A rename gives it a new marble.

  It cannot show:
  - changes inside function bodies;
  - renamed or one-for-one swapped symbols;
  - changed summaries.

  The step's "touched" list knows which files a commit touched, but not what
  changed in them.

  ### The fork's facts
  I counted with `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/6450141c-9e11-
  40e5-95a5-166121c231a3/scratchpad/fork-evidence.mjs`, ignoring shifts in
  source line numbers.

  **Last 20 commits:** 15 touched no file that has a Live Doc; they were records
  and pictures. The other 5 changed 19 files:
  - 14 with no change to their Live Doc's listed facts (e.g. all seven files of
  50ff0ceb, including `palette.ts` and `types.ts`);
  - 2 where the Live Doc changed in facts the sketch doesn't read:
  `slopcop/symbolReferences.ts` in 6d9a8a45 and `history-emphasis.spec.ts` in
  2ab8cabd;
  - 3 that moved the sketch: three e2e specs in 2ba32837.

  **Last 150 commits (Nov 16 to 18):** 37 changed code, touching files 384
  times:
  - 298 (78%) left the Live Doc's facts unchanged;
  - 76 moved the sketch;
  - 10 changed other facts, such as summaries or parameters;
  - 0 changed symbol names without changing the count, so adding names to the
  sketch would have added nothing in this sample.

  **What the whole-file option would take:** a generated line in each Live Doc
  holding 8 numbers, a nearness sketch over the file's text built the same way
  as this one.
  - Every code edit would then rewrite the file's Live Doc, about 4 in 5 more
  doc changes per edit than today.
  - The history's sequence and codec would need to carry the numbers per file
  per step.
  - The line means nothing to a reader.
  - Computing it costs the generator almost nothing.

  A smaller growth is to make the history sequence carry symbol names. The
  evidence says that adds little.

❯ >Item 1 presses on a decision of yours from November 4: arrive on one card at
  reading size, neighbours cut, and learn by zooming out. I won't simply
  reverse it. I'd build the alternative behind a lever (arrive with the
  subject's neighbours' names in view), run the same testers on both, and bring
  you the numbers and both pictures in the app.

  I agree that there is a need to better infer the intent of the user: whether
  it be to probe the deepest specifics of one object, or be it to begin their
  climb along the monkey bars of connections. It might be jarring to always
  start at the zoom required to center the card, and then auto-zooming out to
  "fit", but perhaps that's a reasonable best-of-both-worlds default? The user
  simply will not know that the deepest level of detail exists unless we show
  it to them. It would be a travesty for the entire reader experience to need
  to be a discoverable feature.

  >A World Map box does not name the folder it opens. "payments" opens
  PaymentService/. Every run met it: Opus trusted the box and wrote "likely",
  and two of the smaller-model runs went to search instead.

  I'm glad to finally be getting pushback on the World Map. It's the most
  aesthetically simple and pleasing, but also home for the least defined
  concepts in the whole app. The World Map is presumably where someone goes in
  a blank never-scanned-anything-before Live Documentation Explorer window, and
  that's probably where the first primitive would sit there begging to be
  pointed somewhere, filled with a recursive scan, and then customized with a
  3D shape and intellisense-like assisted labels based on the contents of the
  scan and contents of any other recursively scanned directories brought into
  its playing board by the user.

  >A cycle's returning connection reads as a file linking to itself. Half the
  runs read "1 connection returns in a cycle" with its small loop that way. You
  noted on November 13 that it "looks like a mistake when zoomed out".

  Will need specific screenshots just to make sure we're talking about the same
  thing.

  >Wires say nothing when pointed at. Testers inferred direction from the
  legend's colours and hedged their answers. Both Haiku runs found the plain
  answer in the reader's Dependents list, which no stronger model opened.

  Models live on text. It's natural for them to wish for there to be more. This
  is a bias we must account for. I say this all the time, but I would still
  use MS Teams as a "baseline" standard for UI/UX with tolerable amounts of
  onscreen text in many different viewport sizes. It is possible to take
  complex stuff and make it intuitive enough that words are only warranted on a
  modestly long hover or something of the like. What I don't want to have is
  an application which is shooting random text at me every which way as I
  scroll my mouse across the screen (which it most certainly does in the 3D
  local map -- the most tangled and difficult of our views, but a truly truly
  worthy effort to hone and refine).

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Cells-cost round: handback

  The red case now passes 8 of 8 at one worker and 8 of 8 at two. Its bound and
  its measurement are unchanged. The default stays as it is, and the marks are
  drawn exactly as before. There are two commits in worktree
  `agent-af784aba5d0357850` on top of `a91d7f89`:
  - `7e10d718` holds the pictures and the README in `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSes
  sionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Screenshots/cells-cost/`.
  - `64ad9160` holds the change in
  `packages/explorer/src/client/views/localView/card-factory.ts`, its Live Doc
  with a new authored note, and the regenerated mirror.

  ### Where the time was
  I took a Chrome trace of the case's own press (3D to 2D on `graph.ts`,
  1280×720) and looked at the frame the browser owes after the click:
  - **With Count** the frame takes **59 ms**: about 23 ms of animation-frame
  callbacks and about 31 ms of pre-paint, paint and layerize.
  - **With Cells under name** it took **358 ms**. Of that, **290 ms was 38
  separate `ResizeObserver` callbacks**, one per toolbar. Every encasing
  directory has two toolbars, the line from `createHiddenCells` and the field
  from `createHiddenField`, and each had its own observer. Each callback read
  `clientWidth`/`clientHeight` right after the previous callback had written its
  marks, so each one forced its own style recalc and layout: 232 to 584
  elements, 6 to 12 ms each.
  - So the cost was 38 layouts in a row, not the number of elements and not
  paint.

  What one mark costs to style:
  - Each mark carries an inline style and a `color-mix` marble tint.
  - Each button has a `::before` ring with a transition.
  - That is why each forced pass was expensive. Styling all the marks once costs
  40 to 50 ms.

  Census of the case's map at the reading look:
  - 2,111 elements inside the labels, against 319 with Count.
  - The 18 shown fields hold 372 buttons (all placed), 417 circles, **425 SVG
  `<title>`s** and 29 mask parts.
  - The 18 line toolbars hold 390 more, but they are `display:none` at this
  look, so the browser builds them and never styles them.
  - The map draws no closed directory.

  ### What changed, and why that rather than the alternatives
  1. **One shared `ResizeObserver` for every toolbar of marks** (`observeRoom`).
  It reads every toolbar's room first and then draws them all, so the frame
  lays out once.
     - It reads the same `clientWidth`/`clientHeight` as before, so the drawing
  is identical. I chose those over the observer entry's `contentRect`, whose
  fractional sizes could have moved a mark.
     - Chromium does not keep an observed element alive once it leaves the page.
  I checked: 100 of 100 elements, shown and `display:none`, were
  garbage-collected after removal while the observer lived on. So the shared
  observer leaks nothing.
     - A toolbar out of the page is skipped, as before.
  2. **The marks' SVG no longer carries `<title>`s** (`fieldMarkup` when
  `marked`). That SVG is `aria-hidden` and `pointer-events:none`, and the
  buttons over it already give the names, so those titles were never read. They
  were 425 of the elements styled in that frame.
     - A closed directory's cells keep their titles, which the pointer does
  reach. `closed-directory-looks.spec.ts` checks them and passes.

  After the change the frame takes **133 to 174 ms**: one callback of 19 to 26
  ms, one style recalc of 40 to 50 ms for 1,545 elements, and paint of 35 to 45
  ms.

  Rejected or not needed:
  - **Building only the set the look in force shows.** The hidden set is never
  styled and its draw at width 0 is trivial. Leaving it out would only shorten
  the render, which the bound excludes, and it would need rebuilding on every
  change of look. Not worth the machinery.
  - **Starting the buttons hidden.** This would save about 10 ms, but
  `fillLabel` gives focus back to a mark right away, and a hidden button cannot
  take focus. Rejected.
  - **Lightening the `::before` ring.** That would change the hover fade, and
  how the cells look is not this round.
  - A mark stays one button per file and per directory: pressable, named on
  hover, reached by the arrow keys, and read as "Pin …" or "Open …". Nothing was
  traded away.

  ### The case's numbers
  This is the press's own time (the press's return less the 2D map's render),
  with `--repeat-each=8`:

  | | 1 worker | 2 workers |
  |---|---|---|
  | Before | 547, 565, 572, 582, 591, 608, 715, 878 ms; 0 of 8 passed | 725,
  752, 767, 779, 784, 794, 839, 892 ms; 0 of 8 passed |
  | After | 281, 297, 313, 325, 380, 386, 405, 493 ms; 8 of 8 passed | 288, 291,
  298, 300, 304, 310, 313, 321 ms; 8 of 8 passed |

  - Other builders shared the machine through these runs (load average about 5).
  That is the likely reason the one-worker after-runs spread so widely.
  Earlier, quieter runs gave 268 to 289 ms.
  - With Count you measured 206 to 254 ms, so the cells now cost the press about
  50 to 80 ms where they cost about 350.
  - **The 3D press is close to its own 500 ms bound, separately from this
  round.** It took 374 to 522 ms before (one run of eight over the bound) and
  391 to 497 ms after. I changed nothing on that path.

  ### What I saw in the pictures
  I compared before and after with Hidden Files at Cells under name and at
  Cells, at the reading, far and overview looks, on `pathfind.ts` (this
  repository) and on `PaymentsContext.cs` (the estate, plain and dragged), at
  1440×900.
  - **Marks:** for every toolbar, the marks' SVG (titles set aside), and each
  button's place, size, ring, shown state, hover text, accessible name and
  keyboard stop are identical in all 18 pictures.
  - **Pixels:** the reading and far looks are identical to the pixel, and so is
  the repository's overview.
  - The estate's overview differed by a few hundred pixels of at most 5 levels
  out of 255, and one repository overview run by 2,532 pixels of at most 15. Two
  runs of the same bundle differ in the same way, so this is the overview's own
  run-to-run noise.
  - The dragged pictures can't be compared pixel for pixel, because the same
  drag lands a pixel or two differently from run to run, before as after.
  - One mistake on my side: my first estate comparison was before against
  before, because the estate sample has its own copy of the bundle. I redid it
  against the after bundle; the results above are from the redo.
  - By eye: `history` shows two rows of cells under its name at the reading
  look, and two cells plus a veiled third beside the name at the far look. The
  estate's `PaymentService` shows one dim cell under its chevron, and one after
  its name at the overview. Both match the defaults round's pictures.

  ### Specs run
  - The case alone: 8 + 8 runs before (all failed) and 8 + 8 after (all passed),
  at one and two workers.
  - At one worker after the samples were rebuilt: `hidden-file-cells`,
  `closed-directory-looks`, `closed-directory-families`,
  `local-map-directories`, `local-map-hidden`, `layout-lab`, `scenarios/` and
  the specs that call the design audit on the 2D map (`design-audit`,
  `card-reading`, `descriptions-zoomed-out`, `local-map-pins`,
  `local-map-packing`, `spatial-legend`). **148 passed, 7 skipped, 0 failed.**
  The 7 skips are the scenario table's own "not applicable" skips.
  - Unit tests under `views/localView`: 33 files, 428 tests passed.
  - `npm run lint` is clean. `live-docs:generate` and `live-docs:lint` are clean
  (1,048 files). `slopcop:markdown` found 0 broken links, and
  `docs:links:enforce` is clean.
  - No browser spec was changed.

  ### Forks I decided
  - The shared observer reads the integer `clientWidth`/`clientHeight` rather
  than the observer entry's fractional sizes, so the pixels match.
  - Titles are dropped only from the handle marks' drawing, not from closed
  directories.
  - Each mark stays one button.
  - Building only the set the look in force shows is left out.

  ### Unfinished
  - **Closed directories** have the same structural pattern: one observer per
  box in `observeLayout` (`card-factory.ts` about line 403). In addition,
  `placeTab`, `drawTabs` and `drawCells` each read layout after writing within a
  single box's draw. A map with many closed directories would pay the same kind
  of cost. This map draws none, so I couldn't measure it, and I didn't change
  it. A fix would be the same shared observer plus splitting each draw into a
  read phase and a write phase.

  ### The camera on a cell press (no change made)
  - **A file's cell:** `pinFromHandle` (`controller.ts` about line 791) sets
  `pinHeldDirectory` and goes through the ordinary pin. Then `capturePinChange`
  (about lines 758–773) captures the change as a press on that directory's
  handle, through `captureDirectoryChange`. The handle's label is held still on
  screen and the focus stays among its marks. Afterwards `settleArrival` →
  `settleIntoFrame` (about lines 851–864 and 1571) moves the camera only as far
  as needed to bring the whole picture inside the frame. The camera never goes
  to the new card, so it can land out of view.
  - **A directory's skin:** this goes through `onOpenDirectory` →
  `openRetainedDirectory` → `changeRetainedDirectory` (`index.ts` about line
  761) → `captureDirectoryChange(path)`. Before the press, the subdirectory has
  no label or box of its own, so nothing is held, and only the same settle runs.
  - **Where to change it:** the `afterRender` of `capturePinChange`'s
  held-directory branch and of `captureDirectoryChange`. There the camera could
  be aimed at the new `.node-card[data-id=…]` or the opened directory's label,
  for example the way the centred-subject path sets `focusRequest` and calls
  `settleArrival` (about line 430).

  Housekeeping: the port-8790 server is stopped, the scratchpad probes are
  deleted, and the worktree is clean.

  ## World Map keyboard round: handback

  All six fixes are in **one commit, `a8399d5b`**, on top of `533dcbb0`, in
  worktree
  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-aa7dbd7f336f21a29`. It
  holds the controller, `world.css`, `tests/e2e/world-map.spec.ts`, their three
  Live Docs (regenerated and given authored notes) and the evidence folder.

  **One thing for you before the gate:** this round breaks one case in a file
  outside mine. `tests/e2e/inspection.spec.ts`, "one Escape closes only the
  topmost surface …", fails on the round's build: I ran it. Its line 364 presses
  `?` while the keyboard is on the page body. That press is exactly what fix 1
  stops. To repair it, give the board the keyboard before that press, for
  example `await page.locator("#view-world .world").focus();`. I did not edit
  that file.

  ### What a person now meets, case by case
  I first ran each new case against a bundle built from `533dcbb0`'s World Map
  source, and all six failed for the defect they name.

  1. **Letters only on the board.**
     - What changed: Q, E, T, F, U, G, D, R and ? now listen on `.world`, not on
  the window. They do nothing with Ctrl, Meta or Alt held, or from a field of
  any kind (input, textarea, select, contenteditable). Escape alone stays
  page-wide while the map is the view: it types nothing and it is the way out.
     - How a click gives the board the keyboard: `.world` gets `tabIndex = -1`.
  The browser gives a press to the nearest focusable thing under it, so a press
  on empty board or on a piece focuses `.world`. A press on a tool, link or
  pinned panel keeps the focus the browser gives that, so no control loses
  focus. No listener moves focus. `.world:focus` wears no outline, since it is
  never a Tab stop.
     - Case: "the board's letters act only while the keyboard is on the board
  …". Old failure: "D from the sidebar" switched the theme.
  2. **Reset you can take back.** I kept your design, with one change, explained
  under Forks.
     - After a reset, the positions it discarded are kept until a piece is next
  moved (a drop, or the handle's `move`). Until then, the same tool or R puts
  them back, and that is saved.
     - A reset with nothing moved does nothing.
     - The discarded positions live only for the page. A reload forgets them.
     - Plain words for the owner: "Reset layout now works like a light switch.
  Press it and everything goes back to the board's own layout, and the button
  stays lit. Press it again, or R, and your own arrangement comes back. Once you
  move a piece, the old arrangement is gone and the button resets again."
     - Case: "a reset keeps what it discarded …". Old failure: no
  `aria-pressed`.
  3. **Reduced motion.** `tween` makes the change at once when
  `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` matches. The setting is read at each change,
  so a choice made while the page is open counts. This covers turning, looking
  down and up, and the walkthrough's camera. Fit was already instant.
     - Case: "under reduced motion the camera arrives at once …". It runs with
  the tests' instant handle off. Old failure: one frame after Turn right, the
  camera had hardly turned.
  4. **Help button.** It now has `aria-expanded` and
  `aria-controls="world-help"`, and Escape updates it. Case: "the help button
  says whether the help is open …". Old failure: no `aria-expanded`.
  5. **Walkthrough announced.** A `role="status"` element sits inside `.world`,
  using the existing `visually-hidden` class. It is outside the walkthrough's
  box, which is hidden when the walkthrough ends. Each step writes "Step N of 5.
  …" into it, and finishing clears it. It lives in the World Map's own markup,
  never in `shared/template.html`, so it cannot collide with the page-wide
  region the other builder is adding. Case: "the walkthrough says each step …".
  Old failure: no region.
  6. **Toolbar as one Tab stop.** Roving `tabIndex`: Left and Right arrows wrap,
  Home and End jump to the ends, and the last tool used stays the stop. Case:
  "the toolbar is one Tab stop …". Old failure: nine Tab stops.

  ### Runs
  - **Whole spec on the round's build:** 34 of 35 passed. The failure was "the
  pinned panel stands beside its piece …": unfolded, the panel's bottom sat at
  745.6 px against a 720 px view. I re-ran it twice on each build and it passed
  all four times, so it is an intermittent failure this round did not cause. I
  did not fix it.
  - **Unit tests:** the World Map's own (`views/worldMap`) pass, 53 of 53.
  - **Build:** `npm run build` succeeded.

  ### Forks I decided
  - **Reset's name stays fixed.** You asked that its name and pressed state both
  say which of the two it will do. Rule 5.4 and the APG forbid changing a
  toggle's name while it also says `aria-pressed`. So the accessible name stays
  "Reset layout", `aria-pressed` carries the state, and only the tooltip, which
  becomes the accessible description, changes: "Reset layout (R)" or "Put things
  back where you had them (R)". This strains the rule that "an icon's tooltip
  is its name": the pressed tooltip says what the next press does rather than
  repeating the name. The other way is to keep the tooltip fixed and rely on the
  fill alone.
  - **"The board's surface" means the whole `.world` view.** The letters
  therefore also work with focus on a tool, the pinned panel or the help.
  - **I added one sentence to the help:** "The keys work while the board has the
  keyboard: click it, or Tab to it."

  - The World Map design document has no sentence that became false, so it is
  unchanged.

  Your four answers on the playtests

  1. Arrival. I'm building your suggestion as a trial. It is the "pre-timed
  zoom-out" proposed on November 4 and not selected then. My design is that the
  camera follows the intent a gesture declares:

  - A fresh subject (a link, a search choice): a new Tuning lever, Arrival, with
    three values. Reading Size is today's and stays the default until you've
    seen the others. Reading, Then Neighbours is yours: the card at reading
    size, a held moment, then an eased pull back until its neighbours' names are
    in view. Any input from you stops it where it stands. Neighbours goes
    straight to that last frame.
  - A step from inside the map (a cell pressed, a directory opened): the camera
    keeps its scale and moves only far enough to bring the new card into view,
    with a brief mark on it. This is your wish from yesterday that the camera
    follow a newly opened cell.
  - Why reading size first: it is what a designer calls a signifier, a visible
    cue that something is there to use. Arriving on the full card is the only
    thing that tells a newcomer the reading level exists, which is your
    "travesty" point.

  The testers will then run two tasks on each value: "what uses this file"
  (needs the neighbours) and "what does this symbol do, in its author's words"
  (needs reading size). That measures both of your worries.

  2. The World Map. Your picture of where it goes is in direction.md as yours,
  not as an order to build. I've withdrawn "I'd just build the folder's name
  under the box". After your fourth answer, it belongs on a rested hover and in
  the box's accessible name, not as one more line on the board.

      45 +- **Orientation.** Following a connection, changing scale, open
         +ing evidence and returning must keep recognizable subjects and 
         +context; transitions explain spatial change. Judge motion and w
         +hole journeys, not only stills. Current view behavior and open 
         +designs are in [the Explorer design](.mdmd/layer-3/live-documen

      46  - **Legibility.** Readable names, necessary controls, relevant
          evidence, honest signs of hidden content. Almost no text of our
           own on screen: recognizable icons for repeated actions, with a
          ccessible names, keyboard access, state and hover/focus explana
          tions; optional help behind contextual disclosure. Never shrink
           text to make room. Fade the irrelevant rather than boosting th
          e relevant. No emoji in the UI.
      47  - **Coherence.** Blue identifies what an interface offers; gree
          n what uses it. In the 2D file drawing, uses enter on the left
          and offerings leave on the right. Keep these meanings across sc
          ales. Prefer a symbol's origin file over a barrel re-export whe
          n resolving links.
      48  - **Self-contained page.** The page reads only its own site. Pr
          eserve its CSP and no-network checks, use listeners rather than

● Yes, it is the French Corset, and I should have called it that. The record has
  had the name for this exact drawing since October 5: "a back reference is a
  French Corset pair of laces with a count and its route on hover." The playtest
  findings and my summary said "stub" and "curl", and I never searched the
  record for what the thing already was. That is my miss.

  What you are looking at

  The corset has two uses, and your picture shows the second:

  - The original (December 2025): a file's own symbols referring to each other,
    with both laces on one card.
  - Since October 5: a connection that returns in a cycle, with one lace on each
    of two cards. The green curl at App.config and the blue curl at
    PaymentService.cs are the two ends of one connection.

  Nothing on screen ties the two curls together, which is why even you cannot
  tell. The whole route between them does exist in the page, but it is drawn
  only while a symbol's hover lights it, and that hover is the thing you found
  broken.

  Your three defects

  1. The node-level pins. You asked whether they differ from the earlier
  whole-file pins. They do, and they are drawn alike:

  - The whole-file pin is the early one, your HTML-to-image case. An asset has
    it on both sides. On a card with symbol rows, a whole-file reference arrives
    at the Internals row's dot.
  - The hub was added November 6 for the lines that stand for several links: the
    overview's gathered wires and, since Flow became the default, a pair's line
    at every look. A returning pair's laces end there too.

  On a card's right edge the two are literally one dot. On the left the hub is a
  separate dot at a fixed height, which is why the lace in your picture sits
  level with Estate.Payments.Payme... and reads as that row's. So "I eat the
  whole file" and "I am involved in some way" are not told apart today.

  2. The hover. I have not found the cause yet and won't guess at it. A builder
  is writing the failing case first, then finding the commit that broke it.

  3. The orange. Agreed, and it is being built as you put it: whatever the 3D
  button counts is drawn on the map in the button's own colour, from the same
  count, so the two cannot disagree. One thing to watch is that this amber sits
  close to a directory's orange. If the laces read as directory marks, I'll show
  you the same picture in a second hue.

  What the builder is doing now

  - The hover regression, mended at its cause.
  - The amber on the wires that break the left-to-right order.
  - Pointing at either lace, or at "1 connection returns in a cycle", lights
    both ends and shows the route between them.
  - Each lace named for both its files, with the words shown only after the
    pointer rests.
  - The hub moved up to the height of the file's name, where no row can be
    mistaken for its end, and sized by the look.

  How the two kinds of pin should be told apart is not being built yet. The
  builder is writing down exactly what ends on a hub at each look and what it
  would cost to have a gathered line end in the rows it stands for. I'll bring
  that to you as alternatives in the app.

  step bends one lobe slightly, and the contents' steps can't be told apart by
  eye. In a file of about 2,500 lines, an edit of thirty lines moves the sum by
  roughly thirty over the square root of its size. A contents marble that showed
  every commit would need a sketch tuned to make small edits visible. The
  construction as specified doesn't do that, and the owner should know this
  before choosing.

  this. The passage states the fork as the owner's open question, with the
  counts below.

● The marble trial is on main behind its Tuning lever, with the default
  unchanged, but it does not yet do what you asked for. Its builder drew one
  real file's last ten commits both ways, and in neither row can the eye see a
  marble move from one commit to the next.

  - Why: the pattern is built from a sum scaled to a fixed length, so an edit is
    diluted by the file's size. Thirty lines out of 2,500 change almost
    nothing.
  - The fix, with the builder now: drop that scaling and wrap each component
    through a sine instead. Then one changed line shifts the pattern the same
    small amount whatever the file's size, and hundreds of changes turn it into
    a different marble. I'll choose the sensitivity by eye from its sheets.
  - A side effect I expect: a file with little in it gets a nearly plain marble,
    which should quiet the 3D picture you called "still quite noisy". I haven't
    seen it yet.
  - Your choice, unchanged: whether the pattern comes from what a Live Doc lists
    or from the file's whole contents. Its cost is now counted: about two edits
    in three leave a Live Doc untouched today, and a contents-based pattern
    would rewrite those docs too. I'll put this to you with pictures once the
    fix is in.

  Five builders are still out: this one, the returning connections, the camera,
  the page's frame and the World Map's keyboard. The gate runs once they land.

  ### The intermittent case
  **Cause.** I probed the round's build three times. I opened explorer's pinned
  panel, clicked its files open, and measured the panel's bottom against the
  view's bottom (negative means inside):

  | Moment | Panel bottom vs view bottom |
  | --- | --- |
  | Folded | −13 px |
  | Right after the click | +26 px |
  | After a microtask | +26 px |
  | Inside the next frame's animation-frame callback | +26 px |
  | A frame later | −283 px |

  The panel unfolds where the folded one stood. The old `toggle` listener placed
  it again only in a task of its own, and that task had not run by the time the
  next frame began rendering. Nothing else runs between a frame's callbacks and
  its paint, so that frame showed the panel's bottom 26 px below the view,
  clipped. The case read the box right after its click and caught that state
  about one run in five. The bottom it saw, 745.6 against 720, is the same
  overhang. I ruled out fonts and an easing camera: the camera is instant there,
  and the overhang is the same 26 px in every run.

  **Fix.**
  - Product: a `ResizeObserver` on the panel (disconnected on dispose) replaces
  the `toggle` listener. It places a pinned panel again whenever its size
  changes, and it runs after layout and before paint. It also covers folding,
  and any other change of size.
  - Case: `judge` now waits two frames before measuring, which is the frame a
  person sees.

  **One limit.** The probe cannot see paint itself, so "no painted frame shows
  the overhang under the observer" rests on the browser's rendering order, not
  on a measurement.

  ### Design system document
  I changed only what the round moved. Controller line numbers are as of commit
  3.

  **Rules:**
  - Reversibility: the World Map's reset is added to where it holds and to what
  checks it.

  …".
  - Tab order: the toolbar is one stop where it was nine. The outsider's 18
  stays, as their count.

  never with Ctrl, ⌘ or Alt", and R "and, pressed again before a piece is moved,
  put it back".

  Status, now holds since 2026-11-19, with a short line on what it was before.
  - Escape's place in the World Map: line references updated.
  - The toolbar pattern row (5.3) and the disclosure row (5.3).
  - The walkthrough row in 5.5: now holds, checked by "the walkthrough says each
  step …".
  - 6.3 reduced motion: now holds, checked by "under reduced motion …".

  ## Marble trial, third milestone: the wrap is in, and a commit now shows on a
  marble

  Branch `marble-pattern-turn` was cut from main at 8127eaea and holds one new
  commit, **302bd036** "Wrap the marble sketch through a sine instead of
  bringing it to unit length". Nothing is unfinished.

  With the wrap, a thirty-line commit to a large file is visible at a glance on
  its "from its contents" marble, and one fact is a shift you can find when you
  look for it.

  ### TURN and k
  - **TURN = 4**, `SKETCH_TURN`, chosen by eye from `turn-choice-fork.png` and
  `turn-choice-run.png`.
    - **TURN 2:** a ±41-line commit (Δ1.67) is another marble, and even ±21
  lines recasts it. Too jumpy for one file's own history.
    - **TURN 8:** ±41 lines (Δ0.38) is only a modest shift, and ±4 lines (Δ0.07)
  is barely findable. In the run, everything but "all replaced" stays close to
  the one pattern.
    - **TURN 4:** ±30 to ±46 lines (Δ0.44 to 0.65) is seen at a glance between
  neighbours, and ±4 lines (Δ0.12) is findable. In the run, one symbol is
  slight, five are visible, and all replaced is another marble.
    - Δ here is how far the eight strengths move from the marble before.
  - **k = 0.5**, `SKETCH_STRENGTH`. A large file's components spread evenly
  round the sine, whose root mean square is 1/√2. So 0.5 gives each strength
  1/√8 and the eight together a length of about one, which is the old unit
  length. Vein Bend 0.55 therefore means what it meant.
    - Measured: mean length 0.995 at 500 features, 0.29 at 5, 0.13 for a file
  with no features.
  - **The shader is unchanged.** The wrap is computed per file in the page's
  script, so I didn't measure frames again.

  ### Code
  - **`marbleSketch.ts`:** new `featureSum` (the raw weighted sum),
  `strengthsOf(sum, turn)`, `marbleFeatureSums(graph)`; `sketchOf` and
  `marbleSketches` now wrap. The doc comment carries the reasoning.
  - **`marble-sheet.ts`:** gains `--turns 2,4,8` for the run and fork sheets.
  Fork captions now show two lines: hash and date, then lines added and removed
  with Δ.
  - **Live Docs:** regenerated, with the authored notes updated for the wrap.
  The design document's marble passage is updated.

  ### Unit tests
  `marbleSketch.test.ts` now has these cases:
  - strengths are bounded at 0, 1, 40 and 5,000 features;
  - the same facts give the same sketch in any order;
  - one feature more moves the sketch by about the same at 5, 50 and 500
  features (within a factor of 1.5; the unit length failed this tenfold);
  - the move rises with the number of changed features and saturates near √2;
  - few facts give weak strengths.

  Decision for you

  What the app has tonight is the top row: the pattern is drawn from what a Live
  Doc lists (how many symbols, which files it uses). It gives every file its
  own marble, but a real commit barely moves it. That falls short of your
  "capable of being noticed from commit to commit."

  Only the contents give that. Because the Explorer may show only what the Live
  Docs carry, it means growing the format:

  - One generated line in each Live Doc: eight numbers, a signature of the
    source text that stays close for close texts.
  - Every edit then changes its file's doc by that line. Today about two edits
    in three leave the doc untouched, so doc diffs in a pull request get
    noisier.
  - The history has to carry it per step, and this repository's history must be
    computed again: about 85 minutes by tonight's measurement.

  I recommend the contents. Your wish is about noticing change, and the same
  line would let the docs' own check tell that a doc is stale without
  regenerating it. I have not built it: nothing in the format, the graph or the
  history changed tonight.

› Message from general-purpose
  The type-check failure is mended. The whole non-browser gate passes on it:
  exit 0, nothing red.

  Branch: `runner-typecheck`, from main at 8519e538. One commit: fbd99ab9 "Read
  each browser pass's configuration through a typed function". It cherry-picks
  onto main alone. Working tree clean.

  **Cause, in a sentence.** The gate's "Type-check tests" stage uses
  tests/tsconfig.json, which extends the base. The base sets module and
  moduleResolution to NodeNext, and package.json has no "type": "module", so
  every test file is read as CommonJS. In that mode a dynamic `import()` is left
  as Node's own ESM import. It needs the file's extension, hence TS2307.

  **The config was already in the project.** tests/e2e is included
  (`e2e/**/*.spec.ts` and the rest), so tests/e2e being outside was not the
  cause.

  **Why ".js" alone does not fix it.** Adding the extension makes TypeScript
  find the file, but it then types `.default` as the whole module, which is what
  Node would hand back. Vitest hands back the config, so the two disagree.
  Making it pass would have taken a cast.

  **The mend, with no cast and no ignore.**
  - tests/e2e/playwright.config.ts now exports `configFor(pass)`, a function of
  the pass's name. Its default export is
  `configFor(process.env.LIVE_DOCS_E2E_PASS)`, so Playwright gets exactly what
  it got before.
  - The test imports `configFor` statically and calls it for each pass. It is
  now type-checked against the config's real type, with no environment juggling
  and no module reset.
  - The body of the config only lost its `alonePass` constant; no line of it was
  re-indented.

  **Checks.**
  - `tsc -p tests/tsconfig.json` exits 0.
  - `scripts/e2e-passes.test.ts`: 5/5.
  - ESLint is clean.
  - The real listing still splits the suite: 554 + 5 (main has grown).
  - The case can still fail: a shared pass without its tag filter would put a
  tagged case in both passes, and the case would go red.
  - Live Docs: regenerated under the lock (2 updated), and the two authored
  notes say what changed and why.

  **Gate.** `flock -x /tmp/live-docs-browser.lock npm run safe:commit`, on
  fbd99ab9:
  - Live Docs pipeline, lint, build, type-check tests: passed.
  - Unit: 182 files, 2,053 tests passed.
  - Integration: 21 files, 138 tests passed.
  - Documentation link enforcement: passed.
  - SlopCop markdown: 1,568 files, 0 broken links. Assets: 21 files, 0 broken.
  Symbols: 1,565 files, 0 issues.

❯ >Marble pattern trial

  I tested the marble pattern trial and found that the effect of changes was
  marginal enough as to be not noticeable. What we should prefer is to have
  even a small change in a file produce a _perceptible_ difference.
  Nevertheless, it's quite cool! This is a big step in the right direction!

  One thing I wonder about is whether the concept of "code file = grid of text"
  could be somehow translated into "smoothed grid of text_ure_" with the
  marbles. I don't know. Just curious how to get more interesting temporally
  clear variation effects.

  Oh, and temporal scrubbing wouldn't highlight the pinned note (or wouldn't
  un-dim it), which made watching the changes as I scrubbed past points it
  touched difficult. The history scrubber should either limit itself to only
  that which is salient about the pinned set or at least help snap to those
  salient points of change (including, perhaps by default, jumping only between
  major commit markers in the playback mode and left-right controls of the
  history panel). Combining that with un-dimming the pinned node(s), I think
  will really maximize the viewer's ability to see this system at work.

  ... one more interesting thought related to the capture of an intriguing
  texture from the contents of a scanned file (or the resultant markdown file
  it created, but preferably the scanned file): the "Internals" pseudo-node
  still could benefit from an interesting texture at its inbound connector pin
  (which could also be restyled to have a more rounded-corner box-like shape),
  perhaps also plausibly derived from some shape-like representation of the
  source text. If the inbound connector pin of the internals pseudo-node had a
  bit of that snowflake uniqueness that we have to the marbles, I wonder if
  that might help, if we're ever able to muster it, the ability of the user to
  see the imperceptible differences which don't resize a node across different
  positions of the timeline in the 2D local map view (which we don't have yet
  -- but a consistent history control which lets us go back in time in both 2D
  and 3D is almost certainly where we're headed, design-wise).

  Consider whether you can use the source material of scanned files as a source
  of information that could be textured and, after a while of use,
  imperceptibly allow power-users to detect interesting things which are
  actually salient to the code files at hand. (An example from my day job: I
  took a series of 36 stored procedures, turned them into queries, and
  reformatted them to align semantically similar items by eye, moving all the
  outputs in what would ultimately be a JSON bundle of data over to the exact
  same column position of the sql text file so that a reader could scan these
  queries by eye and find _exactly_ what they're looking for. I aligned the
  joins and their conditions in ways which kept things lined up to the eye. It
  would be absolutely radical if I had some way, at the level of the marble,

  History and the pinned file

  - What the scrubber already knows: the blue marks in your third picture

  The marble

  Your finding matches what I saw on the sheets: drawn from what the Live Doc
  lists, a commit seldom shows. I've recorded your preference for the scanned
  file as the answer to the fork I put to you at 01:37.

  Your "grid of text → texture" idea is what an editor calls a minimap: where
  the ink sits, row by row and column by column. Your aligned queries would show
  as a vertical gutter at the column where the outputs line up. Deep nesting
  shows as a staircase at the left margin, and an image's bytes as an even mess.

  The difficulty is that this picture and your first request pull against each
  other. A picture of a whole file barely moves when one line of 2,500 changes,
  which is the same dilution that made the trial marginal. A hash moves at every
  edit but tells you nothing about the file.

  My proposal is to put two scales in one marble:
  - The page's shape, coarse and slow to change, which is the part you learn to
    read.
  - A fine grain for each band of the page, which turns sharply when that band's
    own lines change. A small edit then shows, and shows roughly where in the
    file it happened.

  The same shape, flat and upright, would be the Internals row's pin as a small
  rounded box, so the 2D card carries it as well.

  The probe will bring back sheets for your eye, with nothing in the product
  yet:
  - three matched versions of the marble, over one file's last ten commits;
  - a real one-line change in a small file;
  - one query written twice, aligned by eye and formatted conventionally;
  - six kinds of file, including an image;
  - a field of forty marbles at the sizes the 3D map draws, to judge whether
    this adds to the noise you named;
  - the Internals pin at its real sizes;
  - a count of how many of 384 real file edits visibly move each version.

  The decision that follows is how a Live Doc carries the shape, because the
  Explorer may only show what the docs hold. My idea is a small picture in the
  doc itself, drawn in Braille or block characters, so a person reading the
  Markdown sees it and git's diff shows which band changed. The probe will show
  samples; nothing is built.

  Your point that one history control across 2D and 3D is where this is headed
  is recorded in direction.

  Where the night stands

● Your question about the marks. They reach exactly one wire out, and no
  further.
  - A tall mark is a commit where the pinned file itself changed shape (created,
    deleted, renamed, its symbol count changed) or where one of its own wires
    appeared, disappeared or changed its count.
  - A short mark is a commit that touched only its text.
  - Not marked: a neighbour changing inside itself.
  - A pinned directory is marked by any file under it.

  The microscope changed what I'm building. An hour ago I planned to make ‹ ›
  mean something different when a file is pinned. Your two knobs are the better
  design, because a button that does one thing in every state needs no
  explaining:
  - Fine: ‹ › stay as they are, one commit each.
  - Coarse: a second pair beside them jumps to the previous or next mark on the
    track. A media player has the same split: frame-step beside skip-to-chapter.
    Page Up and Page Down will do the same as the coarse pair.
  - Play follows the marks when something is pinned.

  The FL Studio zoom: yes, that is the smooth equivalent. It also fits something
  the scrubber already has: the start and end brackets that give a chosen
  stretch the whole track. A wheel or pinch over the track would move those
  brackets continuously around the pointer. As you zoom in, the marks sharpen
  from months to weeks to days to single commits, which is the focus-knob feel
  you describe. I've given it to the same builder as a second milestone after
  the two pairs. It may not land before tonight's gate.

  On "this is stupid, back it out". I can't say that yet, and I can't say the
  opposite either, so I set the test before seeing any result. A stranger gets
  unlabelled marbles cut from the probe's sheets and is asked:
  - which one changed between two commits;
  - which of two is your aligned query, and why;
  - which file is longest;
  - which is not text.

  Each question is scored against chance, for every version and for today's
  marble. I told the builder "none of them, back it out" is an acceptable
  finding. A model failing is not proof your eye would fail; a model succeeding
  is fair evidence the signal is there.

  My lean before the evidence is that the weak half is the 3D marble: it is 20
  to 60 px, turned any way, and dimmed in history. The strong half is the flat,
  upright pin on the 2D card, which is an editor's minimap at thumbnail size.
  Nothing is in your default view either way, since Marble Pattern still
  defaults to One Pattern.

  Your test of the product is being prepared as a playtest. A tester gets only
  the Explorer and is asked how this software came to be. Its account is graded
  against claims drawn from the narrative beforehand. The catch is the one you
  named: our commit subjects are full sentences, so a tester reading them is
  reading words. It runs twice, once with the step panel shown and once hidden.
  The gap between the two scores is what the picture tells without the words. It
  takes the browser only if a run can finish before the gate, otherwise it is
  ready for the next session.

  Six builders are out, with handbacks due by about 04:00 UTC.

› Message from general-purpose
  Text-shape probe: a file's page shape shows what kind of text it is, but no
  candidate let a stranger see which file a commit changed. Committed on branch
  text-shape-probe as 8769bbbb (one commit on 4de93dfc); nothing under
  packages/, nothing generated.

  **Where things are**
  - Code and README:
  AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Probes/text-shape/
    - shape.ts draws the pictures (no browser). run.ts makes the pictures, the
  counts and the samples. trials.ts makes the blind pictures; their answer key
  is trials-key.json.
    - Also there: both SQL queries, numbers.json, touches.json,
  blind-answers.json, and proposal-samples.txt (the Braille and encoded-line
  samples).
  - Sheets and their README: …/2026-11-18/Screenshots/text-shape/ — fork.png,
  small.png, sql.png, kinds.png, field.png, pins.png, pairs-PG.png, pairs-P.png.
  - The sheets have no captions; the README names each tile by position. The
  labelled composer (compose.ts) sat about 15 minutes in an eight-deep queue for
  the browser lane, so I withdrew it and laid the sheets out without a browser.
  The blind reading needed no browser.

  **The three candidates**
  - P: the file's page drawn on the marble, facing the viewer.
  - PG: P plus a fine grain in each band of lines, which turns when a line in
  that band changes.
  - BG: the page wrapped round the marble in the vein colours, with the grain.
  - The page is a 32-column ink grid over the first 128 characters. Rows: one
  per line up to 64; a longer file is squeezed into 64 rows, each the mean of
  its lines. A marble is at most about 60 px on screen, so a short file keeps
  every line and a long file reads as its profile.
  - The grain: each band of lines carries its own wrapped sketch, sin(sum/0.35),
  so one changed line turns that band's grain by about a radian.

  **What I see on each sheet**
  - fork (forceGraphView.ts, last ten commits): P is the same marble at all ten
  commits, because 2,500 lines squeezed into 64 rows keep only the profile. PG
  and BG change only as a mottle with no place you can point to.
  - small (a 58-line test before and after a one-line commit): P looks
  unchanged. PG's grain differs only if you hunt for it.
  - sql: P reads the alignment at a glance — the aliases make a clean vertical
  gutter, while the conventional query's right edge is ragged. PG veils it; BG
  loses it.
  - kinds: in P each kind reads differently. Tests are dense streaks, CSS is
  short lines, JSON shows its indentation staircase, Markdown is long even
  prose, the stored procedure is blocky, and the PNG is an even smear (mostly
  told by its magenta asset colour).
  - field: P is about as calm as today's From Its Doc but has no veins, so it
  loses the marble's meaning. PG is noisier and BG the noisiest.
  - pins: at the real 18×14 size every box is a smudge. At 36×28 the left margin
  shows. At ×4 the page reads.
  - BG left 139 touches pixel-identical, 123 of which moved PG. The likely cause
  is that edits in the first or last band land at the poles, out of view — a BG
  flaw.

  **Numbers (375 touches of the 384; 9 made or removed a file)**

  The measure is the largest mean change in brightness over any 8×8 window of a
  48 px marble, on a 0–255 scale. On the pairs sheets, a change above 8 is
  findable side by side and anything below 5 is invisible to me. Nothing at 48
  px shows at a glance.

  | Candidate | Touches above 8 | Above 4 | Above 1 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |

  ## Text-shape probe

  A file's drawn page reads as a picture of its text, but no texture let a blind
  reader see which file a commit changed. The probe is on branch
  `text-shape-probe`, commit 8769bbbb, built on 4de93dfc. Nothing changed under
  `packages/`, nothing generated, nothing in the Live Doc format. I sent the
  same report to "main" by SendMessage.

  **Where things are**
  - **Probe:**
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Probes/text-shape/`
    - `shape.ts` draws the pictures pixel by pixel, without a browser.
    - `run.ts` makes the pictures, the counts and the samples.
    - `trials.ts` makes the blind reading's pictures, with the answers kept in
  `trials-key.json`; the reader's answers are in `blind-answers.json`.
    - Also there: the two SQL queries, `numbers.json`, `touches.json`,
  `proposal-samples.txt`, and a README.
  - **Sheets:** `…/2026-11-18/Screenshots/text-shape/` holds `fork.png`,
  `small.png`, `sql.png`, `kinds.png`, `field.png`, `pins.png`, `pairs-PG.png`
  and `pairs-P.png`, with a README that says what I see in each.

  The sheets have no captions; the README names each tile by position. The
  shared browser lane had eight jobs queued, and three product rounds were
  waiting on it, so I withdrew the labelled run after about a quarter of an
  hour. The blind reading needed no browser.

  **The candidates**
  - **The page:** a grid of ink, 32 columns over the first 128 characters. Rows:
  one per line up to 64. A longer file is squeezed into 64 rows, each the mean
  of its lines, because a marble is at most about 60 px on screen.
  - **The grain:** each band of lines carries its own sketch, wrapped as
  sin(sum/0.35). One changed line turns its band's grain by about a radian.
  - **P** puts the page inside the ball, facing the viewer. **PG** is P plus the
  grain. **BG** wraps the page round the ball in the vein colours, with the
  grain.

  **What I see on each sheet**
  - **`fork.png`** (forceGraphView.ts over its last ten commits): in P the ten
  balls look the same, since a 2,500-line file keeps only its profile. In PG and
  BG the commits differ only as a mottle with no place you can point to.
  - **`small.png`** (a 58-line test, one line changed): P looks unchanged. In PG
  the change shows only if you hunt for it.
  - **`sql.png`:** P shows the aligned query's alias gutter at a glance, against
  the conventional query's ragged right edge. PG veils it; BG loses it.
  - **`kinds.png`:** in P each kind reads as its own shape.
    - The test is dense streaks; the stylesheet is short lines.
    - The JSON shows its indentation staircase; the Markdown is long even lines.
    - The stored procedure is blocky.
    - The PNG is an even smear, though it is told apart mostly by the asset's
  magenta.
  - **`field.png`** (forty balls at on-screen sizes): P is about as calm as
  today's From Its Doc marble, but it has no veins, so it loses what the marble
  meant. PG is noisier and BG the noisiest.
  - **`pins.png`:** the 2D Internals pin is a smudge at its reading size (about
  18×14). The left margin shows from 36×28, and the page reads at four times
  enlarged.
  - **BG's blind spot:** BG left 139 touches pixel-identical, 123 of which
  changed PG. The likely cause is that edits at a file's start or end fall at
  the ball's poles, out of view. That is a flaw of BG.

  **Numbers**

  Of the 384 touches in the last 150 commits, 375 have the file both before and
  after. The measure is the largest change of brightness in any 8×8 window of a
  48 px ball, on a 0–255 scale. I set the threshold at 8 by eye: above it a
  change can be found side by side, below 5 I could not see one. Nothing at 48
  px shows at a glance.

  | Candidate | Touches above 8 | Above 4 | Above 1 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | P | 56 | 115 | 245 |
  | PG | 171 | 234 | 301 |
  | BG | 186 | 199 | 210 |

  What a Live Doc would carry per file:
  - The page at 2 bits a cell is up to 512 B, and the grain 64 B.
  - As one base64 line: 712 B for the test, 500 B for the SQL, 776 B for
  forceGraphView.ts.
  - As a Braille block in the Markdown: 506, 264 and 627 B. A reader sees the
  page and git's diff shows which band moved, but there is no room for the
  grain.
  - Today 250 of 384 touches leave a Live Doc byte-for-byte unchanged. With the
  page in the doc, nearly every touch would rewrite it.

  "mottled and woven textures … were too uniform to compare ball by ball";
  "small boxes … told me nothing".

  **Verdict**
  - **The grain doesn't work.** It is the texture made to turn at every edit, in
  PG and BG alike. It measures as moving, tells a stranger nothing, and makes
  the field noisier. Back it out.
  - **The page (P) tells a stranger something** and stays calm: it ordered files
  by length twice out of twice, and it showed the hand-aligned query. But it
  answers the "shape of the file" half of the owner's wish, not the "see that a
  commit changed something" half.
  - A model failing to see a change is not proof a person would fail; people
  read texture better, and would learn it over weeks. A model's success is fair
  evidence the signal is there, and the page was the only success.

  **Recommendation**
  - Drop the grain.
  - If anything ships, show the page near up: as an alternative glass on the
  marble, and in the 2D pin at its larger sizes.

  isn't there.
  - For "this file changed here", a plain change mark is honest, which the

  - In the product, P would need to read the page's direction from the view

● Verdict from the texture probe: stop using texture to show change. You invited
  the plain version, and the evidence supports it.

  What failed

  - The grain was my own proposal, and it does not work. It was meant to turn at
    every edit. Measured, it moves at 171 of 375 real file edits, yet it tells
    a viewer nothing.
  - A stranger could not find the changed file. Shown six unlabelled marbles and
    asked which one a commit changed, a fresh model picked right in none of
    five trials with the grain.
  - Today's From Its Doc marble did no better: none of five on the same
    question.
  - The grain adds noise. On the field of forty (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSession
    History/2026/11/2026-11-18/Screenshots/text-shape/field.png), the top two
    rows are the page alone and are calm. The four rows below carry a grain and
    are visibly mottled.
  - No version shows a commit. A 2,500-line file is the same ball at ten
    commits, and one changed line out of 58 changes nothing an eye can find.

  What survived

  The page alone, your "grid of text" idea as an editor's minimap, does tell a
  stranger something:

  - Your aligned queries read. On the query sheet (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessi
    onHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Screenshots/text-shape/sql.png), the top row is
    the query aligned by eye and shows a vertical column where the aliases line
    up. The bottom row is the same query formatted conventionally, with a
    ragged edge. The stranger picked the aligned one and gave that reason.
  - Kinds of file differ. On the kinds sheet (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHis
    tory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Screenshots/text-shape/kinds.png), the first two
    columns show a test, a stylesheet, a JSON file's indentation staircase,
    prose, a stored procedure and an image, each with its own shape.
  - Length reads: three files ordered correctly, twice out of two.

  It only reads near up, at about 100 px on a sphere or about 36 × 28 px flat.
  Most spheres on the 3D map are 20 to 60 px, where it is invisible. At the
  Internals pin's real size of 18 × 14 px, every box is a smudge (AI-Agent-Works
  pace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-18/Screenshots/text-shape/pins.png).

  A model failing is not proof your eye would fail. But I looked at every sheet
  myself and saw the same thing.

  What I recommend

  - Build nothing more on texture. I've filed the page idea in the ideas list
    with this evidence and its cost. The cost is up to about 600 bytes in every
    Live Doc, and nearly every edit of a file would then rewrite its doc.
  - For "a small change should be perceptible", use the mark the history already
    puts on a changed file. It could also say how much changed, by brightness
    or size, if the history carried lines added and removed per commit. I have
    not built this; it is the direction I would try next.
  - The history work now in progress serves the same wish more directly: pinned
    files undimmed, the coarse and fine buttons, and the timeline zoom.

  One question for you

  Do you want Marble Pattern → From Its Doc kept as a look? It is off by default
  and costs nothing while it sits there. It gives each file its own marble but
  does not show change. If you don't say, I'll leave it where it is.

  disagreements: four dates off by a day, two wrong details, and the missing
  package renames of September 27 and 28. I'll verify each against its source
  before correcting the narrative.

❯ I'm looking at the images coming in (sql.png, kinds.png, field.png, pins.png)
  and thinking "don't overliteralize". The text is the substrate but the
  function which extracts shape from it does not need to be so left-dominant or
  literal as the actual column/row positionings remapped as a texture. A text
  file with symmetries should produce a texture with symmetries. A text file
  which is haphazard nonsense should produce a texture which is pseudo-random.
  Just things I'm thinking about. (IDEs use color on different operative word
  types to draw the eye -- there is clearly something structural which the eye
  can detect and suss out).

❯ Super dumb question from me: I almost certainly should already know this 1000
  times over: when we do a history scan, that's only against local git,
  correct? We don't actually reach out to github for that commit history,
  right?

  Listed 1 directory, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Correct: the history scan reads only your local clone and never contacts
  GitHub.

  I checked the code that does it (git.ts
  (packages/engine/src/live-docs/history/git.ts)). Every command it runs is a
  local read: rev-parse, rev-list --first-parent, for-each-ref for tags, diff
  --name-status, and a temporary detached worktree to read each commit's files.
  There is no fetch, pull or remote anywhere in it, and its own header says
  "never a remote, never credentials". Git LFS pointers are left as pointers
  rather than downloaded.

  Two consequences:

  - It sees only what your clone has. Commits you haven't fetched aren't in it,
    and a shallow clone gives a cut-off history, which the scrubber shows as a
    hatched band before the first tick.
  - It follows the current branch's main line only. Work that sat on other
    branches shows up when it was merged, not when it was written. That is why
    the December holiday looks empty on the scrubber even though Dependabot was
    committing on its own branches.

  The page is the same: the Explorer reads the history as a file beside itself
  and loads nothing from the network.

  ### What a person now meets
  - **The pinned file stays visible through the history.** It keeps its ordinary
  look at every step under Changed Files' Dim and Both: its marble, its colour

    - Previous and Next still move one commit.

  marked commit" / "Next marked commit", moves to the nearest mark in that
  direction. With something pinned, a mark is any step that touched it, tall or
  short, at its own step even when a coarser granularity groups the ticks. With
  nothing pinned, it is any drawn tick.
    - The range's two ends are always marks. So where no mark lies ahead the
  pair goes to the end, and each button is disabled only at its own end.
    - Page Up and Page Down now do exactly what the pair does. Before, they
  stopped at tall marks only.
  - **Play.**
    - Before: always one step per tick.
    - Now: with something pinned under the automatic granularity, play jumps
  mark to mark at the play rate, and its tooltip says "Play, one marked commit
  after another".
    - Under an explicit Every Step, Days, Weeks or Months, with nothing pinned,
  or where the pins touched nothing in the range, it still goes one step at a
  time.

  ### Forks I decided
  - **Play also follows the marks under the salient granularity**, not only
  under automatic. That granularity already shows only the marked steps, so
  playing the hidden steps between them would contradict what it shows. This is
  one line in `tick-model.ts` (`plays`) if you want automatic only.
  - **With pins, the marks are the exact steps that touched them**, even where a
  coarser granularity draws a period's tick at the period's last step. This
  follows your wording; the thumb can then land mid-period.
  - **Play's name stays "Play"/"Pause".** It keeps the 2026-11-18 rule for
  toggles; only its tooltip changes. The speed control still says "steps/s",
  which while playing marks means marks per second.
  - **I did not give the coarse buttons an `aria-keyshortcuts` of Page
  Up/Down.** Those keys work only while the slider has focus, so announcing them
  on the buttons would mislead.
  - **Milestone two's arithmetic is partly in place already.** `rangedTickModel`
  already chooses the automatic granularity over the dates inside the range,
  and the stops and play already respect the range's ends.
